ESTABLISHMENT AND MODIFICATION OF NATIONAL FOREST BOUNDARIES
A CHRONOLOGIC RECORD
1891 - 1996
ESTABLISHMENT OF THE NATIONAL FOREST SYSTEM
A Brief History
The National Forest System (NFS) was started when the Nation's public land policy moved from
the disposition to the conservation era. The concept of reserving Federal lands for National Forest
purposes in the late 1800's was driven by the need to protect lands from indiscriminate grazing and
timber-cutting. Passage of two laws dealing with reservation of Federal lands, Section 24 of the Act
of March 30, 1891, and the organic provisions of the 1897 Act, followed two decades of congressional
debate of over 200 bills on public land policy. Section 24 of the 1891 Act was commonly referred
to as the Forest Reserve Act of 1891 and the organic provisions in the 1897 Act are commonly
referred to by the Forest Service as the Organic Administration Act.
The first National Forest, the Yellowstone Park Timber Land Reserve (mostly within the Shoshone
National Forest) in Wyoming, was established by proclamation dated March 30, 1891, and signed
by President Benjamin Harrison under Section 24 of the Act of March 3, 1891, 26 Stat. 1103. He
also signed proclamations creating 14 additional reservations for a gross acreage of 13 million acres.
President James Cleveland created 15 more reserves, with a gross acreage of 25 8 million acres.
Thirteen of the those 15 forest reserves were created by President Cleveland just before he left
office, generating spirited debate and congressional attempts to restore them to the public domain.
Even though the Yellowstone was the first forest reservation in the United States, it is not the
oldest. Approximately fifteen years before the Yellowstone was created, the Spanish Crown created
a Federal Forest Reserve in Puerto Rico which later (established January 17, 1903) became a part
of the Luquillo Forest Reserve and now part of the Caribbean National Forest.
President Theodore Roosevelt, while not responsible for creating the first forest reserves, deserves
his reputation as the pioneer in conserving America's natural resources. He emphasized conservation
in lieu of exploitation. He was responsible for setting aside and reserving many more millions of
acres of public lands for National Forests than did his predecessors. National Forests established
during his Presidency make up the bulk of the 162,820,984 acres of existing National Forests that
were set aside and reserved from the public domain.
Although the 1891 Act gave the President authority to create Forest Reserves, provisions for ad-
ministration of the Reserves were not enacted until Congress provided the organic provisions in
the Act of March 4, 1897. Under the 1897 Act, the Secretary of the Interior was charged with the
administration of the Forest Reserves. These duties, with the exception of certain land disposal
authorities, were turned over to the Secretary of Agriculture by the Transfer Act of February 1,
1905. The Receipts Act of March 4, 1907, changed the name of Forest Reserves to National Forests
and withdrew the President's authority to create National Forests in certain western states.

ESTABLISHMENT OF THE NATIONAL FOREST SYSTEM
A Brief History
(Continued)
The public policy of the Federal Government acquiring lands for protection and administration
gained impetus in the eastern states between 1905 and 1908. During this period, eastern lumber
companies holding large blocks of cutover lands wanted the opportunity to sell their land holdings
to the United States. Public concern was increasing over logging, mining, and farming on fragile
watershed lands. In response to the need to stabilize watershed conditions, Congress enacted the
Weeks Act of March 1, 1911. The Weeks Act provided for the purchase of lands in the watershed
of navigable streams for watershed protection and timber production and provided authority for
the Secretary of Agriculture to designate such lands for permanent retention as National Forests.
Eastern land purchases under the Weeks Act peaked prior to World War II.
During the 1920s and 1930s, agricultural problems were being caused by the cultivation of large
blocks of submarginal farm land that could not profitably grow crops. Personal hardships were com-
monplace and severe droughts, floods, erosion, poor cultivation practices, neglect, and frequently
abandonment were damaging the land. Because of the extent of the problems, the Secretary of
Agriculture set up a National Conference on Land Utilization (LU) in 1931. Under subsequent
emergency relief and industrial recovery acts in the early 1930s and the Bankhead-Jones Farm
Tenant Act of 1937, a total of 11.3 million acres in 253 LU project areas were acquired through
voluntary sales to the United States by the end of the acquisition program in 1946. Many of these
Federal lands were transferred to a variety of other Federal and State agencies for management
as forests, grassland pastures and ranges, park and recreation areas, and wildlife management ar-
eas. From 1938 to 1954, the Soil Conservation Service administered the LU areas retained by the
Secretary of Agriculture except for 1 miffion acres assigned to the Forest Service. On January 2,
1954, the Secretary of Agriculture assigned all of the approximately 8 8 miffion acres of LU project
lands to the Forest Service for management under provisions of the Bankhead-Jones Farm Tenant
Act of 1937. Since that time, an additional 3 5 million acres were transferred to other Federal
and State agencies, 1.5 million acres were given National Forest status, and 3 8 million acres were
permanently set aside as National Grasslands. A record of the establishment and disposition of the
LU projects, and establishment of National Grasslands accompanies this document.
The Land and Water Conservation Fund (L&WCF) Act of 1965 stemmed from the Outdoor Recre-
ation Review Commission's recommendation to provide additional public outdoor recreation land.
The Act earmarks, subject to appropriation, receipts from offshore oil, sale of surplus property, and
recreation user fees, for Federal and State land purchases "to assist in preserving, developing and
assuring accessibility to all citizens of th United States of America of present and future genera-
tions...such quality and quantity of outdoor recreation resources as may be desirable for individual
active participation." The L&WCF is currently the primary method of funding Forest Service land
purchases.
A summary of the NFS lands is updated annually in Table 1 of the "Land Areas of the National
Forest System" (FS-383) report entitled National and Regional Areas Summary.
ELEANOR S. TOWNS
Director of Lands

Preface
This compilation has been made in recognition of the need for a convenient reference list of the
authentic governmental actions under which the individual National Forests were established, their
boundaries subsequently modified, or their status altered. It provides a comprehensive, chronologic
list of official actions affecting Federal Forest Reserves and National Forests, citing the supporting
documents required to trace the legal history of each to November 1996. Land exchanges, dona-
tions, and transfers affecting the National Forest boundaries are not cited because of the number
of actions and the complexity of assembling accurate data. Many forests are grouped together
for administration, but have not been officially consolidated. These changes are not listed here.
Research and experimental areas are also not listed here.
Following the Chronologic Record and Index is Exhibit 1 which includes a listing of special boundary
extension authorities with an alphabetical index of the National Forests. The listing also provides
reference to the citations on which actions affecting a particular National Forest are cited.
Enclosed also are 4 addenda. Addendum 1 discusses the purpose and creation of land utilization
projects. Addendum 2 lists the national grasslands and land utilization projects within the NFS
designated by the Secretary of Agriculture. Addendum 3 summarizes the lands transferred to states
with public use reservations. Addendum 4 lists the lands transferred in land utilization projects
from the Department of Agriculture to other federal departments made prior to assumption of
jurisdiction by the Forest Service and without specific reversionary provisions.
Abbreviations Used

NOTE: The Act of Congress approved June 4, 1897, (30 Stat. 34) contains, among other things, the following proviso: "Provided,
That the Executive orders and proclamations dated February twenty-second, eighteen hundred and ninety-seven, setting apart and
reserving certain lands in the States of Wyoming, Utah, Montana, Washington, Idaho, and South Dakota as forest reservations
be, and they a.re hereby, suspended, and lands embraced therein restored to the public domain the same as though said orders and
proclamations had not been issued: Provided further, That lands embraced in such reservations not otherwise disposed of before
March first, eighteen hundred and ninety-eight, shall again become subject to the operations of said orders and proclamations as
now existing or hereafter modified by the President."

EXHIBIT 1
SPECIAL BOUNDARY EXTENSION AUTHORITIES
Congress extended the provisions of the basic acquisition authorities, primarily the General
Exchange Act of 1922, to lands outside of certain National Forests. These authorities also provide
for extension of the National Forest boundaries around the lands so acquired.

[[page 118]]
LAND UTILIZATION PROJECTS
ADDENDUM 1
Purpose and How Created
In 1934 under an Executive Order, President Roosevelt set up funds in the Federal Emer-
gency Relief Administration for a submarginal land purchase program. The plan called for
the purchase of submarginal agricultural land to be retired from production and made avail-
able for Indian, park, forest, migratory bird refuge and agricultural adjustment purposes.
Provision was made for improving the areas as purchased through PublicWorks that the
lands might be put to the highest use to which they were best suited
In June 1935 all of this activity was transferred to the Resettlement Administration. On
January 1, 1937, the Resettlement Administration was transferred to the Department of
Agriculture and given the designation, the Farm Security AdministrationPrior to this
transfer, all lands earmarked for park purposes had been turned over to the Park Service,
Department of the Interior, for administration On July 22, 1937, the Bankhead-Jones Act
was passed and administration of the Act placed in the Bureau of Agricultural Economics
and on June 9, 1938, the balance of these lands was transferred by Executive Order to the
Bureau of Agricultural Economics for administration under Title III With the reorganization
of the Department of Agriculture in October 1938, the administration was again shifted to
the Soil Conservation Service (SCS) Title III of this Act furnished the first basic legislation
covering the purchase and development of submarginal farm land.
Under Section 31, Title III of the Bankhead-Jones Act stated, "[TJhe Secretary is authorized
and directed to develop a program of land conservation and land utilization, including the
retirement of lands which are submarginal or not primarily suitable for cultivation, in order
thereby to correct inadjustment in land use, and thus assist in controlling soil erosion, refor-
estation, preserving natural resources, mitigating floods, preventing impairment of dams and
reservoirs, conserving surface and subsurface moisture, protecting the watersheds of naviga.
ble streams, and protecting the public lands, health, safety, and welfare." Bankhead-Jones
Title Ill Lands, (50 Stat. 522, 525; 56 Stat. 725; 7 U.s.c. 1011, et seq.)
Most of the Title III land was acquired under authorities other then the above Act, but was
given Title III status by Executive Orders 7908, 10046, and others. These orders were based
on the authority contained in 7 U.S.C. 1019, which section has been repealed.
The administration of Title [II lands was transferred from SCS to the Forest Service on
December 24, 1953, effective January 2, 1954, 19 Federal Register (FR) 74 at 75. Most Title
Ill lands are now included in the National Grasslands, which are a part of the National
Forest System. See regulations at 36 C.F.R. 213. Some Title III lands are now included in
the National Forests; some are still administered by the Forest Service as land utilization
projects; some have been. conveyed to states and other public authorities; some have been
transferred to other government departments; some are under contract of sale or lease to
states; small areas are administered by SCS, 19 FR 467; and small areas are administered
by Agricultural Research Service.
ADDENDUM 1 - 1

[[page 119]]
LAND UTILIZATION PROJECTS
ADDENDUM 1
(Continued)
Title III lands may be conveyed or leased, with or without a consideration, but only to public
authorities and only on condition that the property is used for public purposes (7 U S C
101ic) This condition is enforced by placing a reversionary provision in the deed from the
United States Title III lands may be exchanged to public authorities, as above stated, or
it may be exchanged to public authorities or private parties under the proviso in 7 U S C
101ic without a reversion if land of approximately equal value is received in exchange by the
United States.
Reservations: Until 7 U.SC. 1018 was repealed, in any conveyance, by sale, gift, or exchange,
the United States was required to reserve at least three-fourths of the minerals owned by it
For years, it was the policy to reserve all minerals, except in some conveyances to states
In an exchange involving both acquired land and public domain land, it is necessary to show
separately the land received in exchange for the public domain land, and the land received in
exchange for the acquired land Land received in exchange for public domain land acquires
public domain status This is based on the reasoning in 40 Attorney General 260
The reservation of minerals, timber, or other rights in conveyances to the United States,
should be subjected to the appropriate regulations of the Secretary
The above is not a complete history of Title III lands.It is intended to show how Title
ifi lands caine into being, the types of transfers, acquisitions, and dispositions that have
been made, and how the lands are now administered Addendums 2 through 4 will show the
projects that were transferred to Forest Service custody and control
